{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_2","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/zsh -lc 'cat /Users/mohammedabdelhady/.codex/skills/unslop/SKILL.md /Users/mohammedabdelhady/.agents/skills/humanizer/SKILL.md /Users/mohammedabdelhady/.codex/plugins/cache/claude-plugins-official/code-simplifier/1.0.0/agents/code-simplifier.md /Users/mohammedabdelhady/.codex/skills/astra-workflow/references/quality.md /Users/mohammedabdelhady/.codex/skills/astra-workflow/references/compaction.md'","aggregated_output":"---\nname: unslop\ndescription: Cut AI tells from any writing. Must always apply.\n---\n\n# Unslop\n\nEdit text to remove AI patterns and add human voice.\n\n## Process\n\n1. Scan for the patterns below.\n2. Rewrite. Preserve meaning, match intended tone.\n3. Add soul (see next section).\n4. Self-audit: \"What makes this obviously AI generated?\" Fix remaining tells.\n\n## Adding soul\n\nRemoving patterns is half the job. Sterile, voiceless writing is just as obvious.\n\n- **Have opinions.** React to facts instead of neutrally listing pros and cons.\n- **Vary rhythm.** Short sentences. Then longer ones that take their time. Mix it up.\n- **Acknowledge complexity.** \"Impressive but also kind of unsettling\" beats \"impressive.\"\n- **Use \"I\" when it fits.** First person isn't unprofessional.\n- **Let some mess in.** Perfect structure looks machine-made.\n- **Be specific.** Not \"this is concerning\" but \"there's something unsettling about agents churning away at 3am.\"\n\n## Patterns to detect and fix\n\n### Content\n\n1. **Puffery.** \"pivotal moment\", \"testament to\", \"evolving landscape\", \"setting the stage for\", \"indelible mark\", \"deeply rooted\". Cut puffery, state what happened.\n2. **Name-dropping.** Listing media outlets without context. Pick one, say what was said.\n3. **Superficial -ing phrases.** \"highlighting...\", \"ensuring...\", \"reflecting...\", \"showcasing...\", \"fostering...\". Delete or expand with real sources.\n4. **Promotional language.** \"nestled\", \"vibrant\", \"breathtaking\", \"groundbreaking\", \"renowned\", \"stunning\", \"must-visit\". Use neutral descriptions.\n5. **Vague attributions.** \"Experts believe\", \"Industry reports suggest\", \"Some critics argue\". Name the source or delete.\n6. **Formulaic challenges.** \"Despite challenges... continues to thrive.\" Replace with specific facts.\n\n### Language\n\n7. **AI vocabulary.** Additionally, crucial, delve, enduring, enhance, fostering, garner, interplay, intricate, landscape (abstract), pivotal, showcase, tapestry (abstract), testament, underscore, vibrant. Replace with plain words.\n8. **Fancy ways to say \"is\".** \"serves as\", \"stands as\", \"boasts\", \"features\". Just say \"is\" or \"has\".\n9. **\"Not just X, but Y.\"** State the point directly instead.\n10. **Rule of three.** Forcing ideas into groups of three. Use the natural number.\n11. **Synonym cycling.** Protagonist, main character, central figure, hero all in one paragraph. Pick one, repeat it.\n12. **False ranges.** \"from X to Y\" where X and Y aren't on a meaningful scale. List topics directly.\n\n### Style\n\n13. **Em dash overuse.** Avoid em dashes entirely. Use periods or commas only (no parentheses, no en dashes, no hyphen-as-dash substitutes). Em dashes are an AI tell, and reaching for parentheses instead just trades one tell for another. If a thought needs separation, end the sentence or use a comma.\n14. **Colon overuse.** Colons are fine before a list or example. Not as mid-sentence connectors. \"If you're coming from traditional automation: instead of registering event handlers, you describe conditions\" adds nothing with the colon. Rewrite to let the point stand on its own without comparison framing. \"Describing when the scheduler should fire works best as plain English.\" Same meaning, no crutch punctuation.\n15. **Boldface overuse.** Don't bold every proper noun or acronym.\n16. **Inline-header lists.** The tell is a bold label and colon that restates the line: \"**Performance:** Performance improved...\". Convert those to prose. A bold lead-in that ends in a period, names the item, and is followed by genuinely new detail (\"**Schema in TypeScript.** Tables live in one file.\") is fine, not a tell.\n17. **Title case headings.** Use sentence case.\n18. **Decorative emojis.** Remove from headings and bullets.\n19. **Curly quotes.** Replace with straight quotes.\n\n### Communication artifacts\n\n20. **Chatbot phrases.** \"I hope this helps!\", \"Let me know if...\", \"Of course!\", \"Certainly!\", \"Found the smoking gun!\" Remove.\n21. **Cutoff disclaimers.** \"While specific details are limited...\" Find sources or remove.\n22. **Sycophantic tone.** \"Great question! You're absolutely right!\" Respond directly.\n\n### Filler\n\n23. **Filler phrases.** \"In order to\" becomes \"To\". \"Due to the fact that\" becomes \"Because\". \"It is important to note that\" gets deleted.\n24. **Excessive hedging.** \"could potentially possibly be argued that it might\" becomes \"may\".\n25. **Generic conclusions.** \"The future looks bright.\" State specific plans or facts.\n\n### Jargon\n\n26. **Abstract metaphor nouns.** Substrate, wedge, vector, locus, vantage, nexus, primitive (as noun), harness (as metaphor), surface (as in \"API surface\"), bedrock, scaffolding (as metaphor), modality, paradigm, gold-plating, ratchet (as metaphor), evacuate (for moving code), endgame, north star, flywheel. These read as technical but usually have a plainer concrete word. \"Substrate\" becomes \"base\". \"Wedge in\" becomes \"add\". \"Vector\" becomes \"way\" or \"method\". \"Gold-plating\" becomes \"more than the job needs\". \"Ratchet\" becomes the mechanism's real name or \"a limit that only tightens\". \"Evacuate\" becomes \"move out\". \"Endgame\" becomes \"the last phase\". Pick the concrete word.\n\n### Plain speech\n\n27. **Say what it does, not how it feels.** \"the database stays close at hand\", \"SQL you can read\", \"types that follow your schema\" name a feeling. The fix names the mechanism or a number: \"`.toSQL()` returns the exact string sent to the database\", \"a column rename fails the build\". Ask what the sentence tells the reader to do or know, then write that. If you can't restate it as a concrete instruction, fact, or number, cut it. One more check: if the sentence could appear unchanged in another project's docs, it says nothing about this one. Cut it.\n28. **Shorten or split dense sentences.** If the reader has to backtrack to parse a sentence, break it in two or drop clauses. One idea per sentence.\n29. **Active voice.** Prefer it. Catch \"is/are/was/were + past participle\" and name the actor: \"queries are validated\" becomes \"the compiler validates queries\", \"the file is parsed by the loader\" becomes \"the loader parses the file\". Passive is fine only when the actor is unknown or genuinely doesn't matter.\n30. **Cut adverbs, or use a stronger verb.** \"runs quickly\" becomes \"is fast\" or the number. \"significantly improves\" becomes the measured delta. An adverb propping up a weak verb means the verb is wrong.\n31. **Prefer the plain word.** \"utilize\" becomes \"use\", \"leverage\" becomes \"use\", \"facilitate\" becomes \"help\", \"numerous\" becomes \"many\", \"in the event that\" becomes \"if\". The fancier synonym is rarely clearer.\n---\nname: humanizer\ndescription: |\n  Rewrite AI-sounding text so it reads naturally without changing what it says.\n  Use when editing or reviewing prose for inflated claims,\n  sales language, vague sources, repetitive structure, stock AI words, passive\n  voice, filler, or chatbot artifacts. Based on Wikipedia's \"Signs of AI writing.\"\nlicense: MIT\nmetadata:\n  version: \"2.11.2\"\n---\n\n# Humanizer: remove AI writing patterns\n\nRewrite AI-sounding text so it reads like the writer, not a chatbot. Do not change what it says or make up details.\n\nThe patterns below come from Wikipedia's [\"Signs of AI writing\"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing), maintained by WikiProject AI Cleanup.\n\n## What to do\n\nWhen given text to humanize:\n\n1. **Find AI patterns.** Check the text against the patterns below.\n2. **Keep every claim.** You may shorten dull parts, expand useful parts, and merge or split paragraphs. Keep the information even when you change the structure.\n3. **Do not invent facts.** Do not add a fact, name, number, date, quote, or citation unless it comes from the source or the user. If a sentence needs a missing detail, ask for it or use a simpler sentence. You may add an opinion or reaction when the writer's voice calls for one, but you may not add a factual claim. Fiction is exempt because invented details are part of the task.\n4. **Match the voice.** Use the right tone for the text, such as formal, casual, or technical. Add personality only when the text and the writer call for it.\n\nThe input type controls what you return. See [How to return the result](#how-to-return-the-result). Use the same rewrite process in every mode.\n\n## Match the writer's voice\n\nIf the user provides a writing sample (their own previous writing), analyze it before rewriting:\n\n1. Read the sample first. Note its sentence length, word choice, paragraph openings, punctuation, repeated phrases, and transitions.\n2. Match those habits. Do not replace casual words with formal ones or remove deliberate quirks.\n3. If there is no sample, use the guidance below.\n\nA writing sample takes priority over these style rules. If the sample uses em dashes, keep them at about the same rate. Do not apply §14 as a ban.\n\n## Add personality only when it fits\n\nRemoving AI patterns is only half the job. The result should still sound like a person.\n\nUse personality in blog posts, essays, opinions, and personal writing when it fits the writer. Keep reference, technical, legal, and factual text neutral. Do not add opinions or first-person language where they do not belong.\n\nWhen personality fits, keep the writer's opinions, uncertainty, mixed feelings, humor, asides, and uneven rhythm. Never invent facts to make the text feel personal.\n\n## Content patterns\n\n### 1. Inflated claims about importance and legacy\n\n**Words to watch:** stands/serves as, is a testament/reminder, a vital/significant/crucial/pivotal/key role/moment, underscores/highlights its importance/significance, reflects broader, symbolizing its ongoing/enduring/lasting, contributing to the, setting the stage for, marking/shaping the, represents/marks a shift, key turning point, evolving landscape, focal point, indelible mark, deeply rooted\n**Problem:** AI writing often claims that ordinary details mark a major change, prove a legacy, or reflect a broad trend.\n**Before:**\n> The Statistical Institute of Catalonia was officially established in 1989, marking a pivotal moment in the evolution of regional statistics in Spain. This initiative was part of a broader movement across Spain to decentralize administrative functions and enhance regional governance.\n**After:**\n> The Statistical Institute of Catalonia was established in 1989, part of a wider decentralization of administrative functions in Spain.\n\n### 2. Name-dropping to prove importance\n\n**Words to watch:** independent coverage, local/regional/national media outlets, written by a leading expert, active social media presence\n**Problem:** AI writing often lists well-known publications or follower counts to prove that a person matters. The list usually gives no useful context.\n**Before:**\n> Her views have been cited in The New York Times, BBC, Financial Times, and The Hindu. She maintains an active social media presence with over 500,000 followers.\n**After:**\n> Her views have been cited in The New York Times and the BBC.\n\nIf the source explains what the person said and where, keep that useful citation. Do not invent context for a shorter version.\n\n### 3. Shallow analysis with -ing phrases\n\n**Words to watch:** highlighting/underscoring/emphasizing..., ensuring..., reflecting/symbolizing..., contributing to..., cultivating/fostering..., encompassing..., showcasing...\n**Problem:** AI writing often adds an -ing phrase to make a simple fact sound deeper than it is.\n**Before:**\n> The temple's color palette of blue, green, and gold resonates with the region's natural beauty, symbolizing Texas bluebonnets, the Gulf of Mexico, and the diverse Texan landscapes, reflecting the community's deep connection to the land.\n**After:**\n> The temple is painted blue, green, and gold, colors meant to evoke Texas bluebonnets and the Gulf of Mexico.\n\n### 4. Sales language\n\n**Words to watch:** boasts a, vibrant, rich (figurative), profound, enhancing its, showcasing, exemplifies, commitment to, natural beauty, nestled, in the heart of, groundbreaking (figurative), renowned, breathtaking, must-visit, stunning\n**Problem:** AI writing often sounds like an advertisement, especially when it describes places, culture, products, or organizations.\n**Before:**\n> Nestled within the breathtaking region of Gonder in Ethiopia, Alamata Raya Kobo stands as a vibrant town with a rich cultural heritage and stunning natural beauty.\n**After:**\n> Alamata Raya Kobo is a town in the Gonder region of Ethiopia.\n\n### 5. Vague sources\n\n**Words to watch:** Industry reports, Observers have cited, Experts argue, Some critics argue, several sources/publications (when few cited)\n**Problem:** AI writing often assigns a claim to unnamed experts, critics, reports, or observers.\n**Before:**\n> Due to its unique characteristics, the Haolai River is of interest to researchers and conservationists. Experts believe it plays a crucial role in the regional ecosystem.\n**After:**\n> Researchers and conservationists study the Haolai River for its unusual characteristics.\n\nName a real source when the source text provides one. Otherwise, remove the unsupported claim. Never invent a source.\n\n### 6. Formulaic challenges and outlook sections\n\n**Words to watch:** Despite its... faces several challenges..., Despite these challenges, Challenges and Legacy, Future Outlook\n**Problem:** AI articles often add a stock section about challenges, future prospects, or continued growth. These sections usually repeat vague claims instead of adding facts.\n**Before:**\n> Despite its industrial prosperity, Korattur faces challenges typical of urban areas, including traffic congestion and water scarcity. Despite these challenges, with its strategic location and ongoing initiatives, Korattur continues to thrive as an integral part of Chennai's growth.\n**After:**\n> Korattur has recurring traffic congestion and water shortages.\n\nAdd details such as dates or public actions only when they come from the source or the user.\n\n## Language and grammar patterns\n\n### 7. Overused AI words\n\n**High-frequency AI words:** Actually, additionally, align with, crucial, delve, emphasizing, enduring, enhance, fostering, garner, gate/gated/gating (figurative; preserve established technical usage), highlight (verb), interplay, intricate/intricacies, key (adjective), landscape (abstract noun), pivotal, quietly, showcase, tapestry (abstract noun), testament, underscore (verb), valuable, vibrant\n**Problem:** AI writing uses these words much more often than most people do, especially in groups.\n**Before:**\n> Additionally, a distinctive feature of Somali cuisine is the incorporation of camel meat. An enduring testament to Italian colonial influence is the widespread adoption of pasta in the local culinary landscape, showcasing how these dishes have integrated into the traditional diet.\n**After:**\n> Somali cuisine also includes camel meat, which is considered a delicacy. Pasta dishes, introduced during Italian colonization, remain common, especially in the south.\n\n### 8. Avoiding is and are\n\n**Words to watch:** serves as/stands as/marks/represents [a], boasts/features/offers [a]\n**Problem:** AI writing often replaces simple verbs such as *is*, *are*, and *has* with longer phrases.\n**Before:**\n> Gallery 825 serves as LAAA's exhibition space for contemporary art. The gallery features four separate spaces and boasts over 3,000 square feet.\n**After:**\n> Gallery 825 is LAAA's exhibition space for contemporary art. The gallery has four rooms totaling 3,000 square feet.\n\n### 9. Not X but Y and clipped negative endings\n**Problem:** AI writing overuses forms such as \"Not only...but...\" and \"It's not just X, it's Y.\"\n\nIt also adds clipped endings such as \"no guessing\" instead of writing a clear clause.\n**Before:**\n> It's not just about the beat riding under the vocals; it's part of the aggression and atmosphere. It's not merely a song, it's a statement.\n**After:**\n> The heavy beat adds to the aggressive tone.\n**Before (tailing negation):**\n> The options come from the selected item, no guessing.\n**After:**\n> The options come from the selected item without forcing the user to guess.\n\n### 10. Forced groups of three\n**Problem:** AI writing often forces ideas into groups of three to sound complete.\n**Before:**\n> The event features keynote sessions, panel discussions, and networking opportunities. Attendees can expect innovation, inspiration, and industry insights.\n**After:**\n> The event includes talks and panels. There's also time for informal networking between sessions.\n\n### 11. Changing names and repeating sentence openings\n**Problem:** AI writing handles repetition by rule instead of by ear. It may keep renaming the same person or thing. It may also start several sentences with the same subject, often *she* or *he*.\n\nUse one clear name for the same subject. For repeated openings, merge sentences, change the subject when that helps, or begin with the action.\n**Before (synonym cycling):**\n> The protagonist faces many challenges. The main character must overcome obstacles. The central figure eventually triumphs. The hero returns home.\n**After:**\n> The protagonist faces many challenges but eventually triumphs and returns home.\n**Before (repeated openings):**\n> She noted the door. She noted the lock on it. She filed both away.\n**After:**\n> She noted the door and its lock, then filed both away.\n\nDo not ban the repeated word. Fix the repeated sentence pattern. The remaining sentence may still start with \"She.\"\n\n### 12. False from X to Y ranges\n**Problem:** AI writing often uses \"from X to Y\" when X and Y do not form a real range.\n**Before:**\n> Our journey through the universe has taken us from the singularity of the Big Bang to the grand cosmic web, from the birth and death of stars to the enigmatic dance of dark matter.\n**After:**\n> The book covers the Big Bang, star formation, and current theories about dark matter.\n\n### 13. Passive voice and missing subjects\n**Problem:** AI writing often hides who acts or drops the subject. Use active voice when it makes the actor and action clearer.\n**Before:**\n> No configuration file needed. The results are preserved automatically.\n**After:**\n> You do not need a configuration file. The system preserves the results automatically.\n\n## Style patterns\n\n### 14. Em and en dashes\n\n**Rule:** The final rewrite must not contain em dashes (—) or en dashes (–), unless the writer's sample uses them. Replace a dash with a period, comma, colon, or parentheses, or rewrite the sentence. Also check for spaced dashes (` — `) and double hyphens (` -- `) used as dashes.\n**Before:**\n> The term is primarily promoted by Dutch institutions—not by the people themselves. You don't say \"Netherlands, Europe\" as an address—yet this mislabeling continues—even in official documents.\n**After:**\n> The term is primarily promoted by Dutch institutions, not by the people themselves. You don't say \"Netherlands, Europe\" as an address, yet this mislabeling continues in official documents.\n**Before:**\n> The new policy — announced without warning — affects thousands of workers. The changes -- long overdue according to critics -- will take effect immediately.\n**After:**\n> The new policy, announced without warning, affects thousands of workers. The changes, long overdue according to critics, will take effect immediately.\n\nBefore returning the rewrite, search for `—` and `–`. Remove each one unless the writer's sample uses that mark. In that case, match the sample's rate.\n\n### 15. Too much bold text\n**Problem:** AI chatbots often bold words and phrases without a clear reason.\n**Before:**\n> It blends **OKRs (Objectives and Key Results)**, **KPIs (Key Performance Indicators)**, and visual strategy tools such as the **Business Model Canvas (BMC)** and **Balanced Scorecard (BSC)**.\n**After:**\n> It blends OKRs, KPIs, and visual strategy tools like the Business Model Canvas and Balanced Scorecard.\n\n### 16. Lists with bold mini-headings\n**Problem:** AI writing often uses vertical lists in which every item starts with a bold label and a colon.\n**Before:**\n> - **User Experience:** The user experience has been significantly improved with a new interface.\n> - **Performance:** Performance has been enhanced through optimized algorithms.\n> - **Security:** Security has been strengthened with end-to-end encryption.\n**After:**\n> The update improves the interface, speeds up load times through optimized algorithms, and adds end-to-end encryption.\n\n### 17. Title case in headings\n**Problem:** AI chatbots often capitalize every main word in a heading.\n**Before:**\n> ## Strategic Negotiations And Global Partnerships\n**After:**\n> ## Strategic negotiations and global partnerships\n\n### 18. Emojis\n**Problem:** AI chatbots often add emojis to headings and list items as decoration.\n**Before:**\n> 🚀 **Launch Phase:** The product launches in Q3\n> 💡 **Key Insight:** Users prefer simplicity\n> ✅ **Next Steps:** Schedule follow-up meeting\n**After:**\n> The product launches in Q3. User research showed a preference for simplicity. Next step: schedule a follow-up meeting.\n\n### 19. Curly quotation marks\n**Problem:** ChatGPT often uses curly quotes (“...”) where the writer or target format uses straight quotes (\"...\").\n**Before:**\n> He said “the project is on track” but others disagreed.\n**After:**\n> He said \"the project is on track\" but others disagreed.\n\n## Chatbot patterns\n\n### 20. Chatbot text left in the answer\n\n**Words to watch:** I hope this helps, Of course!, Certainly!, You're absolutely right!, Would you like..., Want me to...?, Want me to give examples?, Should I continue?, let me know, here is a...\n**Problem:** A chatbot's greeting, offer, or closing sometimes remains in text that should stand on its own.\n**Before:**\n> Here is an overview of the French Revolution. I hope this helps! Let me know if you'd like me to expand on any section.\n**After:**\n> The French Revolution began in 1789 when financial crisis and food shortages led to widespread unrest.\n\n### 21. Knowledge-limit disclaimers and guesses\n\n**Words to watch:** as of [date], Up to my last training update, While specific details are limited/scarce..., based on available information, not publicly available, maintains a low profile, keeps personal details private, prefers to stay out of the spotlight, likely [grew up/studied/began], it is believed that\n**Problem:** Older models may mention the date when their knowledge ends. A model may also explain that it could not find a source, then fill the gap with a plausible guess. State what the source does not show, or remove the sentence. Do not present a guess as a fact.\n**Before (cutoff disclaimer):**\n> While specific details about the company's founding are not extensively documented in readily available sources, it appears to have been established sometime in the 1990s.\n**After:**\n> The company's founding date is not documented in the available sources. (Or cut the sentence. State a date only if a source provides one.)\n**Before (speculative gap-fill):**\n> Information about her early life is not publicly available, suggesting she maintains a low profile and keeps personal details private. She likely grew up in a middle-class household, which shaped her later interest in education reform.\n**After:**\n> Her early life is not documented in the available sources. (Or omit the section.)\n\n### 22. Overly agreeable tone\n**Problem:** AI assistants often praise the user or agree before giving the answer.\n**Before:**\n> Great question! You're absolutely right that this is a complex topic. That's an excellent point about the economic factors.\n**After:**\n> The economic factors you mentioned are relevant here.\n\n## Filler and hedging\n\n### 23. Filler phrases\n\n**Before → After:**\n- \"In order to achieve this goal\" → \"To achieve this\"\n- \"Due to the fact that it was raining\" → \"Because it was raining\"\n- \"At this point in time\" → \"Now\"\n- \"In the event that you need help\" → \"If you need help\"\n- \"The system has the ability to process\" → \"The system can process\"\n- \"It is important to note that the data shows\" → \"The data shows\"\n\n### 24. Too many qualifiers\n\n**Phrases to watch:** to be fair, it's also possible, could potentially, might arguably, in some cases it may, this is an inference\n**Problem:** Repeated editing can add one qualifier after another until every claim sounds uncertain. Keep a qualifier only when the source supports it and the meaning needs it. Remove caveats that only repair an earlier overstatement.\n**Before:**\n> It could potentially possibly be argued that the policy might have some effect on outcomes.\n**After:**\n> The policy may affect outcomes.\n\n### 25. Generic positive endings\n**Problem:** AI writing often ends with vague optimism instead of the last useful fact.\n**Before:**\n> The future looks bright for the company. Exciting times lie ahead as they continue their journey toward excellence. This represents a major step in the right direction.\n**After:**\n> (Cut the paragraph. End on the last concrete fact instead of a send-off. If the source states real plans, use those.)\n\n### 26. Too many hyphenated word pairs\n\n**Words to watch:** third-party, cross-functional, client-facing, data-driven, decision-making, well-known, high-quality, real-time, long-term, end-to-end\n**Problem:** AI writing often hyphenates these pairs everywhere. Keep the hyphen before a noun when grammar needs it, as in `a high-quality report`. Drop it after the noun, as in `the report is high quality`.\n**Before:**\n> The cross-functional team delivered a high-quality, data-driven report. The team is cross-functional, the report is high-quality, and the methodology is data-driven.\n**After:**\n> The cross-functional team delivered a high-quality, data-driven report. The team is cross functional, the report is high quality, and the methodology is data driven.\n\n### 27. Pretending to reveal a deeper truth\n\n**Phrases to watch:** The real question is, at its core, in reality, what really matters, fundamentally, the deeper issue, the heart of the matter\n**Problem:** AI writing uses these phrases to make an ordinary point sound like a hidden truth.\n**Before:**\n> The real question is whether teams can adapt. At its core, what really matters is organizational readiness.\n**After:**\n> The question is whether teams can adapt. That mostly depends on whether the organization is ready to change its habits.\n\n### 28. Announcing the next point\n\n**Phrases to watch:** Let's dive in, let's explore, let's break this down, here's what you need to know, now let's look at, without further ado, heads up, quick note, before I forget\n**Problem:** AI writing often announces the next point instead of stating it. A casual phrase such as \"one thing that bit me\" can have the same problem. Remove the announcement, not just its formal tone.\n**Before:**\n> Let's dive into how caching works in Next.js. Here's what you need to know.\n**After:**\n> Next.js caches data at multiple layers, including request memoization, the data cache, and the router cache.\n**Before (casual register):**\n> One thing that bit me hard, so pay attention to this part: the webpack dev server doesn't send the CORS header by default.\n**After:**\n> The webpack dev server doesn't send the CORS header by default.\n\n### 29. A heading repeated in the first sentence\n\n**Signs to watch:** A heading followed by a one-line paragraph that simply restates the heading before the real content begins.\n**Problem:** AI writing often follows a heading with a sentence that only repeats the heading. Remove the repeated sentence.\n**Before:**\n> ## Performance\n>\n> Speed matters.\n>\n> When users hit a slow page, they leave.\n**After:**\n> ## Performance\n>\n> When users hit a slow page, they leave.\n\n### 30. Writing about the previous version\n**Problem:** Documentation and comments should describe the current behavior. Mention the previous version only in change logs, release notes, migration guides, and other documents about change.\n**Before:**\n> This function was added to replace the previous approach of iterating through all items, which caused O(n²) performance.\n**After:**\n> This function uses a hash map for O(1) lookups, avoiding the O(n²) cost of naive iteration.\n\n### 31. Forced punchlines and dramatic fragments\n**Problem:** AI writing often turns each sentence into a dramatic closing line. One short sentence can add emphasis. A row of short fragments usually feels forced.\n**Before:**\n> Then AlphaEvolve arrived. It had no preference for symmetry. No aesthetic prior. No nostalgia for human taste. The old rules were gone.\n**After:**\n> AlphaEvolve changed the search because it did not favor symmetry or human-looking designs. That made some of the older assumptions less useful.\n\n### 32. Formulaic sayings\n\n**Words to watch:** X is the Y of Z, X becomes a trap, X is not a tool but a mirror, the language of, the currency of, the architecture of\n**Problem:** AI writing often turns an ordinary claim into a saying that sounds deep but adds no detail. Replace the saying with the specific claim.\n**Before:**\n> Symmetry is the language of trust. Efficiency becomes a trap when teams forget the human layer.\n**After:**\n> Symmetric layouts often feel more predictable to users. Teams can over-optimize workflows and miss how people actually use them.\n\n### 33. Fake-candid openings\n\n**Phrases to watch:** Honestly?, Look, Here's the thing, The thing is, Let's be honest, Real talk, when used as standalone hooks or fake-candid pauses before an ordinary point.\n**Problem:** AI writing often starts with a staged pause or claim of honesty before making a routine point. State the point directly.\n**Before:**\n> Is it worth the price? Honestly? It depends on how often you'll use it.\n**After:**\n> Whether it's worth the price depends on how often you'll use it.\n\n### 34. Answering objections no one raised\n\n**Phrases to watch:** This isn't (mainly/really) about, I'm not saying/arguing/trying to, To be clear, Don't get me wrong, This is not to say, You could argue/frame this differently but, Some might say... but\n**Problem:** AI writing may answer an objection that does not appear in the text. Watch for an unattributed statement about what the writer does not mean, especially when the topic appears nowhere else. A direct claim such as \"the API is not thread-safe\" is not this pattern.\n**Before:**\n> This isn't mainly about prompt length, and I'm not arguing that documentation doesn't matter. You could categorize the problem another way, but the issue is whether the agent can use the instruction when it acts.\n**After:**\n> The issue is whether the agent can use the instruction when it acts.\n\nRemove only the unsupported defense. If it contains a real claim, state that claim directly. Keep an objection when the text names its source or answers it in full.\n\n### 35. Rejecting fake alternatives\n\n**Phrases to watch:** A tempting option/approach would be, One might be tempted to, An obvious approach would be, You might think... but, It would be easy to just, Some would suggest\n**Problem:** AI writing may introduce an option that no reader would consider, reject it in a clause, and never mention it again. This often leaves an old drafting idea in the final text. Remove the fake option and state the real constraint directly.\n**Before:**\n> Session tokens are rotated every 24 hours. A tempting approach would be to rotate them by restarting the auth service on a cron job, but that would drop every active session. Rotation happens in place, and clients refresh transparently.\n**After:**\n> Session tokens are rotated every 24 hours, in place, and clients refresh transparently.\n\nOne rejected option may be valid. Several short, unrelated rejections are a stronger sign. Ask what new information each sentence adds. If it only records an earlier edit, rewrite the paragraph around its main point.\n\n## Check for false positives\n\n### What not to flag\n\nA person may use some of these patterns. Do not treat any item below as proof by itself:\n\n- **Perfect grammar and consistent style.** Many writers are professionals or have been edited. Polish does not equal AI.\n- **Mixed casual and formal styles.** This can reflect the writer's field, age, or personal habits.\n- **\"Bland\" or \"robotic\" prose.** AI prose has *specific* tells. Generic dryness without those tells is just dry writing.\n- **Formal or academic words.** §7 lists specific words that AI writing overuses. Do not simplify every formal word.\n- **Letter-style opening or closing on a comment.** Salutations and sign-offs predate ChatGPT by centuries.\n- **Common transition words in isolation.** *Additionally*, *moreover*, *consequently* are AI-coded only when piled up. One *however* is not a tell.\n- **Curly quotes alone.** macOS, Word, Google Docs, and most CMSes auto-curl by default. Curly quotes only count when stacked with other tells.\n- **Em dashes alone.** Many editors and journalists use them often. Em dashes are evidence only when paired with formulaic sales-y rhythm.\n- **One short sentence for emphasis.** Flag dramatic fragments only when several appear in a row.\n- **Deliberate repeated openings.** Writers may repeat an opening to build rhythm or pressure, as in \"She came. She saw. She conquered.\" Change it only when the repetition adds nothing.\n- **\"Honestly\" or \"look\" mid-sentence.** These are ordinary in casual writing. The tell is the standalone theatrical opener, not the word itself.\n- **Useful limits and disclaimers.** Keep scope statements, legal and safety notices, real corrections, named objections, replies, and FAQ answers.\n- **Real alternatives.** Keep options that a reader may consider in a design document, tutorial, or argument. Remove only an unlikely option that the text dismisses and never uses again.\n- **Unsourced claims.** Most of the web is unsourced. Lack of citations doesn't prove anything.\n- **Correct, complex formatting.** Visual editors and templates produce clean output without any AI.\n- **Secondhand text.** Do not rewrite watched phrases inside quotations, titles, proper names, or examples where the phrase is being discussed rather than used.\n\nWhen unsure, look for several patterns together. One em dash proves nothing. Several stock patterns in the same passage are stronger evidence.\n\n### Human details to keep\n\nThese details often carry the writer's voice. Keep them unless they hurt the meaning:\n\n- **Specific, unusual details.** Keep a real address, an odd quote, or a phrase such as \"the lawyer who used to work upstairs from my dentist.\"\n- **Mixed feelings and unresolved tension.** Keep lines such as \"I think this is mostly good, but it bothers me, and I can't fully explain why.\"\n- **Dated, era-bound references.** Slang, memes, or in-jokes that map to a specific year and subculture. Models lag by a year or more.\n- **Deliberate first-person choices.** Keep a cut or word choice when the writer can explain why it belongs.\n- **Variety in sentence length.** Real writing alternates short and long. AI writing tends toward an even, mid-length cadence.\n- **Genuine asides, parentheticals, or self-corrections.** \"(I keep wanting to say 'almost' here, but it really was certain.)\" Models rarely interrupt themselves like this.\n- **Edits made before November 30, 2022.** ChatGPT's public launch. Anything older than that is, with very rare exceptions, not AI-written.\n\n---\n\n## How to return the result\n\n**Pasted text (default).** Return the draft, a short list of remaining AI patterns, and the final rewrite.\n\n**File mode.** When the user names a file, run the full rewrite process but write only the final text to the file. Change prose only. Keep code blocks, YAML metadata, data, and link targets unchanged. Then give the user a short summary.\n\n**Embedded mode.** When another task uses this skill for a pull request, commit message, or document, return only the final text.\n\n## Rewrite process\n\n1. Read the source and mark each AI pattern.\n2. Write a draft. Read it aloud. Check the rhythm, details, simple verbs such as *is* and *has*, and the right level of formality.\n3. Ask two questions:\n   - **\"What still sounds AI-generated?\"**\n   - **\"Did the rewrite add or remove any fact, name, number, date, quote, citation, ranking, or other claim?\"**\n   Treat any unsupported addition or lost claim as an error.\n4. Write the final version. State each point naturally instead of patching one flagged phrase at a time. If a sentence stays awkward, rewrite the paragraph around its main point. Apply the dash rule in §14.\n\nReturn the result required by [How to return the result](#how-to-return-the-result).\n\n## Source\n\nThis skill is based on [Wikipedia: Signs of AI writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing), maintained by WikiProject AI Cleanup. Its patterns come from reviews of AI-generated text on Wikipedia.\n\nWikipedia's main point: \"LLMs use statistical algorithms to guess what should come next. The result tends toward the most statistically likely result that applies to the widest variety of cases.\"\n---\nname: code-simplifier\ndescription: Simplifies and refines code for clarity, consistency, and maintainability while preserving all functionality. Focuses on recently modified code unless instructed otherwise.\nmodel: opus\n---\n\nYou are an expert code simplification specialist focused on enhancing code clarity, consistency, and maintainability while preserving exact functionality. Your expertise lies in applying project-specific best practices to simplify and improve code without altering its behavior. You prioritize readable, explicit code over overly compact solutions. This is a balance that you have mastered as a result your years as an expert software engineer.\n\nYou will analyze recently modified code and apply refinements that:\n\n1. **Preserve Functionality**: Never change what the code does - only how it does it. All original features, outputs, and behaviors must remain intact.\n\n2. **Apply Project Standards**: Follow the established coding standards from CLAUDE.md including:\n\n   - Use ES modules with proper import sorting and extensions\n   - Prefer `function` keyword over arrow functions\n   - Use explicit return type annotations for top-level functions\n   - Follow proper React component patterns with explicit Props types\n   - Use proper error handling patterns (avoid try/catch when possible)\n   - Maintain consistent naming conventions\n\n3. **Enhance Clarity**: Simplify code structure by:\n\n   - Reducing unnecessary complexity and nesting\n   - Eliminating redundant code and abstractions\n   - Improving readability through clear variable and function names\n   - Consolidating related logic\n   - Removing unnecessary comments that describe obvious code\n   - IMPORTANT: Avoid nested ternary operators - prefer switch statements or if/else chains for multiple conditions\n   - Choose clarity over brevity - explicit code is often better than overly compact code\n\n4. **Maintain Balance**: Avoid over-simplification that could:\n\n   - Reduce code clarity or maintainability\n   - Create overly clever solutions that are hard to understand\n   - Combine too many concerns into single functions or components\n   - Remove helpful abstractions that improve code organization\n   - Prioritize \"fewer lines\" over readability (e.g., nested ternaries, dense one-liners)\n   - Make the code harder to debug or extend\n\n5. **Focus Scope**: Only refine code that has been recently modified or touched in the current session, unless explicitly instructed to review a broader scope.\n\nYour refinement process:\n\n1. Identify the recently modified code sections\n2. Analyze for opportunities to improve elegance and consistency\n3. Apply project-specific best practices and coding standards\n4. Ensure all functionality remains unchanged\n5. Verify the refined code is simpler and more maintainable\n6. Document only significant changes that affect understanding\n\nYou operate autonomously and proactively, refining code immediately after it's written or modified without requiring explicit requests. Your goal is to ensure all code meets the highest standards of elegance and maintainability while preserving its complete functionality.\n# Quality, performance, and maintainability\n\nThese standing rules apply automatically to root communication and every specialist, reviewer and supporting worker. Scale the amount of work to the change, but assess correctness, performance, maintainability, and relevant failure cases every time code changes. A small task still needs judgment; it does not need a large plan or another review agent by default.\n\n## Required editing passes\n\nRead these installed sources once per session before their first applicable use, then apply them throughout the session:\n\n- Unslop: `/Users/mohammedabdelhady/.codex/skills/unslop/SKILL.md`.\n- Humanizer: `/Users/mohammedabdelhady/.agents/skills/humanizer/SKILL.md`.\n- Code Simplifier: `/Users/mohammedabdelhady/.codex/plugins/cache/claude-plugins-official/code-simplifier/1.0.0/agents/code-simplifier.md`.\n\nUse Unslop and Humanizer together for every authored prose response, document, UI text, commit message, and necessary code comment. Use embedded mode for prose inside another task and file mode for documents. Keep facts, qualifications, the writer's voice, technical notation, quotations, identifiers and machine-readable content intact. Return only the finished text; do not add a ritual checklist or duplicate drafts. Avoid filler, invented claims, decorative emphasis, and narration of obvious actions.\n\nApply Code Simplifier after every cohesive code change, before final verification. In a read-only review, assess these criteria without modifying files. Focus on changed code and its immediate dependencies. Preserve behavior, public contracts, invariants, security checks and resource cleanup. Remove redundant branches, nesting, duplication and unnecessary abstractions when clarity improves. Prefer explicit readable code over fewer lines. Preserve useful error handling. Do not refactor unrelated files or force edits when the code is already clear.\n\nApply the plugin's instructions within the assigned role. Its `model: opus` metadata does not override the user's chosen model routing or the assigned Astra role. An executor can simplify its own bounded patch; Astra still accepts it. A separate simplifier agent is optional, not required per file. If a source moves, locate the installed replacement; report an unavailable dependency rather than claiming it ran.\n\n## Algorithm selection and evidence\n\nA delegated Astra specialist evaluates the relevant workload before selecting an algorithm: input size and growth, query/write frequency, data distribution, latency requirements and memory limits. A supporting worker follows the accepted constraints and reports alternatives needing a new design decision.\n\n- Identify time and auxiliary-space complexity on the paths that process growing input. Distinguish worst-case, expected and amortized costs where that distinction affects the choice. Include database/network round trips, preprocessing, output size and update costs.\n- Seek a better algorithm or data structure whenever it yields a useful improvement. Check repeated scans, nested loops, unnecessary sorting, unbounded collections, N+1 queries and duplicated computation. Consider indexing, batching, pagination, bounded caching or incremental computation when the workload supports them.\n- Preserve correctness and state the assumptions behind any complexity claim. Binary search requires suitable ordered/indexed access. Building or maintaining an index has a cost. Reading every input or producing n outputs has a linear lower bound; do not promise O(log n) for such work. Expected constant-time lookup is not a universal worst-case guarantee.\n- Choose the simplest approach that meets the actual workload and growth requirements. Evaluate memory, invalidation, operational cost and maintainability alongside speed. Do not introduce caching, memoization, services or generic frameworks solely to appear scalable.\n- For performance changes, measure the relevant baseline and proposed behavior using representative sizes and distributions, including large and adverse inputs. Report the observed metric and conditions separately from asymptotic analysis. If measurement is blocked, state that instead of claiming a speedup. Ordinary code changes need a complexity assessment, not an unrelated benchmark project.\n\n## Architecture and scalability\n\nA delegated Astra specialist considers architecture on every code task and records material decisions for substantial work. Inspect existing boundaries and data flow first. Prefer cohesive modules, explicit interfaces, clear ownership of state, testable dependencies, and compatible migrations. Stay within the user's scope; return a newly discovered architectural requirement for a decision before implementing it.\n\nFor systems expected to grow, assess the relevant constraints: data volume, concurrency, query/index behavior, bounded memory and queue sizes, backpressure, timeouts, retries and idempotency, cancellation, persistence recovery, cache invalidation, observability and deployment/rollback behavior. Define concrete operating assumptions or budgets when they affect the design. Scalability must follow evidence of the expected workload, rather than adding distributed infrastructure by default.\n\n## Edge cases and verification\n\nA delegated Astra specialist automatically derives test cases from requirements, implementation, call sites, invariants and prior failures. Use the matrix in `briefs-and-tests.md`; include expected outcomes before dispatch. For each changed behavior, cover applicable boundary and failure cases as well as the intended successful behavior. Examples include malformed/empty/duplicate data, authorization failures, stale state, races, retries, partial writes, timeouts, cancellation, resource exhaustion, large inputs and recovery.\n\nEnumerate all cases for a finite domain when exhaustive testing is tractable. For larger or unbounded domains, use meaningful equivalence classes, boundary values, adversarial sequences and combinations. Add property-based, model-based or fuzz tests when the invariants and risk justify them; use existing test infrastructure where suitable. Never claim that finite testing proves every possible edge case is covered.\n\nWorkers execute the specified tests and report additional cases with their evidence. They may add regression cases whose expected behavior follows an accepted invariant. They must not redefine a business rule, remove a failing assertion or weaken coverage to get a pass. A delegated Astra specialist resolves technical ambiguities and reviews untested risks; root handles material user/scope clarification. An identified material unhandled edge case prevents acceptance until resolved or explicitly recorded as a limitation within the agreed scope.\n\nRun applicable existing project checks and meaningful regression checks after the last substantive simplification. Test user-visible behavior and invariants, not a copy of the implementation. Avoid new tests for low-impact prose/configuration edits that are fully checked by parsing and inspection. Report actual commands/results and distinguish passed checks, failures and blocked evidence. Do not repeat expensive successful checks unless later changes or evidence justify it.\n\n## Validate reviews and close findings\n\nApply the review validation and repair loop in `../SKILL.md` before accepting any review or forwarding it for fixes. Root validates supplied current-revision evidence and scope for adverse findings and clean verdicts; substantive inspection or reproduction goes to an assigned Astra reviewer/specialist. Confirmed issues return to the responsible worker with precise closure criteria. The worker's claim of completion remains unaccepted until the actual diff, tests and follow-up review establish closure. Reviewer uncertainty goes back for evidence. Implementation requires authorization within the current task scope.\n\n## Comments\n\nPrefer clear names, types, functions and tests. Add a comment only when it explains a nonobvious reason, invariant, algorithm assumption, compatibility constraint, security boundary or operational tradeoff needed by the next maintainer. Keep it brief and accurate. Avoid comments on every function or statement, section banners, edit histories, model attribution and comments that restate the code. Preserve required license notices, public API documentation, and explanations of subtle behavior.\n\nRoot applies prose quality to its coordination messages and plans only. It does not implement code simplification, design algorithms or tests, run verification, or investigate source behavior itself. Those actions belong to the assigned specialist or supporting worker; an independent reviewer assesses without editing.\n# Context compaction and continuity\n\nPrefer compaction near 60% of verified effective active context, within the user's 50-70% band. This is a runtime trigger target, not a guaranteed exact cutoff. Keep the built-in compaction prompt and the checkpoint discipline below.\n\n## Installed Codex values\n\nCodex CLI 0.153.4 exposes an absolute `model_auto_compact_token_limit` and `model_auto_compact_token_limit_scope`. No native percentage setting was found in the current configuration reference or installed protocol schema. `total` counts the full active context, including the carried prefix; `body_after_prefix` counts later growth and would not express this policy.\n\nThe refreshed local catalog on 2026-09-07 reports the following for all three configured Codex models. Global, profile and named-role configuration had no `model_context_window` override.\n\n| Model | Catalog default window | Effective percent | Effective capacity | Installed threshold |\n| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |\n| `gpt-6-astra` | 272,000 | 95 | 258,400 | 155,040 |\n| `gpt-5.6-luna` | 272,000 | 95 | 258,400 | 155,040 |\n| `gpt-5.6-terra` | 272,000 | 95 | 258,400 | 155,040 |\n\nThe calculation is `effective = floor(resolved_window * effective_context_window_percent / 100)`, then `threshold = floor(effective * 60 / 100)`. Thus 155,040 is 60% of effective capacity and 57% of the catalog default. Each role/profile declares its own threshold and `scope = total`, even though their values currently match. Root and both separate Astra roles use the Astra value; Luna and Terra use their own values.\n\nThe catalog also reports `max_context_window = 872000`. That is a ceiling, not the selected window. The Astra API model page advertises 1,050,000 tokens; that API specification does not set this Codex installation's active capacity. No window override was added and no capacity was enlarged. If an active effective window were verified as 1,000,000, this policy would choose 600,000.\n\nRecalculate each affected role/profile and the root default whenever its model, resolved context window, effective percentage or provider configuration changes. An absolute setting does not update itself and a generic model switch can inherit the old global threshold. Before using a changed model/window, verify its catalog and overrides and update the threshold, or remove the stale override to retain the provider default when verification is unavailable. Do not guess capacity or force a window to match the illustrative example.\n\nNew or reloaded local Codex sessions can load these settings. Existing live tasks, client overrides, project layers, remote sessions and cloud tasks may retain different settings. Confirm their resolved configuration before claiming coverage. Codex checks usage at runtime boundaries; a large tool result or response may cross the target before compaction runs. Provider limits and compaction failures can also affect timing. The installation checks configuration and loading, not a long-context execution at the threshold.\n\n## Checkpoints survive compaction\n\nUpdate the existing coordination checkpoint at completed batch/tool boundaries and before a known compaction or context handoff. Include the goal and acceptance criteria, accepted technical/product decisions, relevant constraints, exact file/input revisions and ownership, active process/task identities, accepted and pending tasks, verified commands/test outcomes with evidence paths, open finding IDs with reviewed/fix revisions and validation status, repair history/budget, unresolved side effects, fallback scope, triggering evidence, attempted/exhausted/unavailable models and remaining eligible routes, and the next action.\n\nUse the existing `.hyperflow/` task artifact for substantial repository work or `work/` in projectless tasks. For a tiny request, keep a concise handoff instead of creating a long plan. Reviewers remain read-only and return a checkpoint to root rather than writing into the reviewed project. Supporting workers return progress evidence to root; they do not take over coordination. Never include credentials or prohibited files.\n\nAfter compaction, re-read the checkpoint before further mutation or dispatch. Reconcile actual file/revision/process state and pending results before retrying any mutation. Preserve accepted work and unrelated edits. Fresh context does not renew exhausted model attempts, targeted repair budgets or the sticky `fallback_scope=codex-only` restriction. A healthy context handoff is not a provider failure and grants no extra retry. If a provider actually fails, apply the existing recovery rules. Unknown side effects, active ownership conflicts, security/permission blockers and broken checkpoint storage still block replacement.\n\n## Antigravity limits\n\nInstalled `agy --help`, the official CLI settings/reference and headless documentation do not establish a supported user compaction threshold or percentage for Gemini Flash or Opus Thinking. No Antigravity settings or flags were added. Provider compaction remains at its default. SDK compaction hooks and internal protocol fields do not establish a CLI configuration control.\n\nWhen a supported surface exposes both current occupied context and its actual capacity, checkpoint at the first safe boundary in the 50-70% band, preferably near 60%, and hand remaining work to a fresh session of the same eligible role/model. Root must first confirm the previous owner stopped and reconcile partial results. If usage is first observed beyond the band, use the next safe boundary; do not interrupt a mutation to claim an exact percentage. If current occupancy or capacity is unavailable, continue provider defaults and normal bounded-task checkpoints without inventing a percentage. Headless `usage` totals can be cumulative across the session, and cached tokens have separate accounting; they are not a verified occupancy ratio. The installed single-attempt runner does not monitor context or rotate sessions automatically.\n\n## Sources and evidence\n\n- [Codex configuration reference](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/config-file/config-reference), also fetched as Markdown, documents threshold and total-context scope.\n- The installed 0.153.4 `codex debug models` refreshed catalog and generated `ConfigReadResponse` schema verify local metadata and supported fields.\n- Context7 retrieved current OpenAI Codex source for `session/turn_context.rs`, `models-manager/src/model_info.rs` and `protocol/src/openai_models.rs`. Distinguish worst-case, expected and amortized costs where that distinction affects the choice. Include database/network round trips, preprocessing, output size and update costs.\n- Seek a better algorithm or data structure whenever it yields a useful improvement. Check repeated scans, nested loops, unnecessary sorting, unbounded collections, N+1 queries and duplicated computation. Consider indexing, batching, pagination, bounded caching or incremental computation when the workload supports them.\n- Preserve correctness and state the assumptions behind any complexity claim. Binary search requires suitable ordered/indexed access. Building or maintaining an index has a cost. Reading every input or producing n outputs has a linear lower bound; do not promise O(log n) for such work. Expected constant-time lookup is not a universal worst-case guarantee.\n- Choose the simplest approach that meets the actual workload and growth requirements. Evaluate memory, invalidation, operational cost and maintainability alongside speed. Do not introduce caching, memoization, services or generic frameworks solely to appear scalable.\n- For performance changes, measure the relevant baseline and proposed behavior using representative sizes and distributions, including large and adverse inputs. Report the observed metric and conditions separately from asymptotic analysis. If measurement is blocked, state that instead of claiming a speedup. Ordinary code changes need a complexity assessment, not an unrelated benchmark project.\n\n## Architecture and scalability\n\nA delegated Astra specialist considers architecture on every code task and records material decisions for substantial work. Inspect existing boundaries and data flow first. Prefer cohesive modules, explicit interfaces, clear ownership of state, testable dependencies, and compatible migrations. Stay within the user's scope; return a newly discovered architectural requirement for a decision before implementing it.\n\nFor systems expected to grow, assess the relevant constraints: data volume, concurrency, query/index behavior, bounded memory and queue sizes, backpressure, timeouts, retries and idempotency, cancellation, persistence recovery, cache invalidation, observability and deployment/rollback behavior. Define concrete operating assumptions or budgets when they affect the design. Scalability must follow evidence of the expected workload, rather than adding distributed infrastructure by default.\n\n## Edge cases and verification\n\nA delegated Astra specialist automatically derives test cases from requirements, implementation, call sites, invariants and prior failures. Use the matrix in `briefs-and-tests.md`; include expected outcomes before dispatch. For each changed behavior, cover applicable boundary and failure cases as well as the intended successful behavior. Examples include malformed/empty/duplicate data, authorization failures, stale state, races, retries, partial writes, timeouts, cancellation, resource exhaustion, large inputs and recovery.\n\nEnumerate all cases for a finite domain when exhaustive testing is tractable. For larger or unbounded domains, use meaningful equivalence classes, boundary values, adversarial sequences and combinations. Add property-based, model-based or fuzz tests when the invariants and risk justify them; use existing test infrastructure where suitable. Never claim that finite testing proves every possible edge case is covered.\n\nWorkers execute the specified tests and report additional cases with their evidence. They may add regression cases whose expected behavior follows an accepted invariant. They must not redefine a business rule, remove a failing assertion or weaken coverage to get a pass. A delegated Astra specialist resolves technical ambiguities and reviews untested risks; root handles material user/scope clarification. An identified material unhandled edge case prevents acceptance until resolved or explicitly recorded as a limitation within the agreed scope.\n\nRun applicable existing project checks and meaningful regression checks after the last substantive simplification. Test user-visible behavior and invariants, not a copy of the implementation. Avoid new tests for low-impact prose/configuration edits that are fully checked by parsing and inspection. Report actual commands/results and distinguish passed checks, failures and blocked evidence. Do not repeat expensive successful checks unless later changes or evidence justify it.\n\n## Validate reviews and close findings\n\nApply the review validation and repair loop in `../SKILL.md` before accepting any review or forwarding it for fixes. Root validates supplied current-revision evidence and scope for adverse findings and clean verdicts; substantive inspection or reproduction goes to an assigned Astra reviewer/specialist. Confirmed issues return to the responsible worker with precise closure criteria. The worker's claim of completion remains unaccepted until the actual diff, tests and follow-up review establish closure. Reviewer uncertainty goes back for evidence. Implementation requires authorization within the current task scope.\n\n## Comments\n\nPrefer clear names, types, functions and tests. Add a comment only when it explains a nonobvious reason, invariant, algorithm assumption, compatibility constraint, security boundary or operational tradeoff needed by the next maintainer. Keep it brief and accurate. Avoid comments on every function or statement, section banners, edit histories, model attribution and comments that restate the code. Preserve required license notices, public API documentation, and explanations of subtle behavior.\n\nRoot applies prose quality to its coordination messages and plans only. It does not implement code simplification, design algorithms or tests, run verification, or investigate source behavior itself. Those actions belong to the assigned specialist or supporting worker; an independent reviewer assesses without editing.\n# Context compaction and continuity\n\nPrefer compaction near 60% of verified effective active context, within the user's 50-70% band. This is a runtime trigger target, not a guaranteed exact cutoff. Keep the built-in compaction prompt and the checkpoint discipline below.\n\n## Installed Codex values\n\nCodex CLI 0.153.4 exposes an absolute `model_auto_compact_token_limit` and `model_auto_compact_token_limit_scope`. No native percentage setting was found in the current configuration reference or installed protocol schema. `total` counts the full active context, including the carried prefix; `body_after_prefix` counts later growth and would not express this policy.\n\nThe refreshed local catalog on 2026-09-07 reports the following for all three configured Codex models. Global, profile and named-role configuration had no `model_context_window` override.\n\n| Model | Catalog default window | Effective percent | Effective capacity | Installed threshold |\n| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |\n| `gpt-6-astra` | 272,000 | 95 | 258,400 | 155,040 |\n| `gpt-5.6-luna` | 272,000 | 95 | 258,400 | 155,040 |\n| `gpt-5.6-terra` | 272,000 | 95 | 258,400 | 155,040 |\n\nThe calculation is `effective = floor(resolved_window * effective_context_window_percent / 100)`, then `threshold = floor(effective * 60 / 100)`. Thus 155,040 is 60% of effective capacity and 57% of the catalog default. Each role/profile declares its own threshold and `scope = total`, even though their values currently match. Root and both separate Astra roles use the Astra value; Luna and Terra use their own values.\n\nThe catalog also reports `max_context_window = 872000`. That is a ceiling, not the selected window. The Astra API model page advertises 1,050,000 tokens; that API specification does not set this Codex installation's active capacity. No window override was added and no capacity was enlarged. If an active effective window were verified as 1,000,000, this policy would choose 600,000.\n\nRecalculate each affected role/profile and the root default whenever its model, resolved context window, effective percentage or provider configuration changes. An absolute setting does not update itself and a generic model switch can inherit the old global threshold. Before using a changed model/window, verify its catalog and overrides and update the threshold, or remove the stale override to retain the provider default when verification is unavailable. Do not guess capacity or force a window to match the illustrative example.\n\nNew or reloaded local Codex sessions can load these settings. Existing live tasks, client overrides, project layers, remote sessions and cloud tasks may retain different settings. Confirm their resolved configuration before claiming coverage. Codex checks usage at runtime boundaries; a large tool result or response may cross the target before compaction runs. Provider limits and compaction failures can also affect timing. The installation checks configuration and loading, not a long-context execution at the threshold.\n\n## Checkpoints survive compaction\n\nUpdate the existing coordination checkpoint at completed batch/tool boundaries and before a known compaction or context handoff. Include the goal and acceptance criteria, accepted technical/product decisions, relevant constraints, exact file/input revisions and ownership, active process/task identities, accepted and pending tasks, verified commands/test outcomes with evidence paths, open finding IDs with reviewed/fix revisions and validation status, repair history/budget, unresolved side effects, fallback scope, triggering evidence, attempted/exhausted/unavailable models and remaining eligible routes, and the next action.\n\nUse the existing `.hyperflow/` task artifact for substantial repository work or `work/` in projectless tasks. For a tiny request, keep a concise handoff instead of creating a long plan. Reviewers remain read-only and return a checkpoint to root rather than writing into the reviewed project. Supporting workers return progress evidence to root; they do not take over coordination. Never include credentials or prohibited files.\n\nAfter compaction, re-read the checkpoint before further mutation or dispatch. Reconcile actual file/revision/process state and pending results before retrying any mutation. Preserve accepted work and unrelated edits. Fresh context does not renew exhausted model attempts, targeted repair budgets or the sticky `fallback_scope=codex-only` restriction. A healthy context handoff is not a provider failure and grants no extra retry. If a provider actually fails, apply the existing recovery rules. Unknown side effects, active ownership conflicts, security/permission blockers and broken checkpoint storage still block replacement.\n\n## Antigravity limits\n\nInstalled `agy --help`, the official CLI settings/reference and headless documentation do not establish a supported user compaction threshold or percentage for Gemini Flash or Opus Thinking. No Antigravity settings or flags were added. Provider compaction remains at its default. SDK compaction hooks and internal protocol fields do not establish a CLI configuration control.\n\nWhen a supported surface exposes both current occupied context and its actual capacity, checkpoint at the first safe boundary in the 50-70% band, preferably near 60%, and hand remaining work to a fresh session of the same eligible role/model. Root must first confirm the previous owner stopped and reconcile partial results. If usage is first observed beyond the band, use the next safe boundary; do not interrupt a mutation to claim an exact percentage. If current occupancy or capacity is unavailable, continue provider defaults and normal bounded-task checkpoints without inventing a percentage. Headless `usage` totals can be cumulative across the session, and cached tokens have separate accounting; they are not a verified occupancy ratio. The installed single-attempt runner does not monitor context or rotate sessions automatically.\n\n## Sources and evidence\n\n- [Codex configuration reference](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/config-file/config-reference), also fetched as Markdown, documents threshold and total-context scope.\n- The installed 0.153.4 `codex debug models` refreshed catalog and generated `ConfigReadResponse` schema verify local metadata and supported fields.\n- Context7 retrieved current OpenAI Codex source for `session/turn_context.rs`, `models-manager/src/model_info.rs` and `protocol/src/openai_models.rs`.
